      "content": "\\section{Method}\n\n\\subsection{Task Definition}\n\nWe study \\emph{verdict prediction}: given a Python function, a test input, and an optional execution trace, predict whether the test passes or fails.\nThe model produces a single token---\\texttt{pass} or \\texttt{fail}---making this a binary classification task.\nUnlike code generation \\citep{chen2025tracepile} or output prediction \\citep{gu2024cruxeval}, verdict prediction isolates the model's ability to detect faults from its ability to simulate execution, providing a cleaner lens on what trace information actually contributes.\n\n\\subsection{Dataset}\n\\label{sec:dataset}\n\nWe sampled 10 candidate solutions per problem from Qwen-2.5-Coder-7B-Instruct (temperature 0.8, top\\_p 0.95) for 591 problems from HumanEval \\citep{chen2021evaluating} (164 problems) and MBPP-sanitized \\citep{austin2021program} (427 problems), executed each solution against canonical test cases with \\texttt{sys.settrace} to obtain line-level execution traces and pass/fail verdicts, yielding 5,910 trace--verdict pairs (67\\% pass, 33\\% fail).\nThe dataset is split 80/20 by problem ID into 4,720 training and 1,190 test samples; the test set majority-class baseline is 71.51\\%.\n\nThe \\texttt{exception\\_only} format collapses multiple passing solutions into identical rows (39.45\\% train, 48.99\\% test duplicates) because correct executions produce no exception events; train--test input overlap is zero (no leakage).\nFull deduplication statistics are in Appendix~\\ref{sec:dedup_stats}.\n\n\\subsection{Trace Representations}\n\nWe define seven input conditions that systematically vary the trace information available to the model (Figure~\\ref{fig:trace_overview}):\n\n\\begin{itemize}\n\\item \\texttt{no\\_trace}: Source code and test input only; no execution information.\n\\item \\texttt{full\\_trace}: Complete line-by-line execution trace recording variable states at each step.\n\\item \\texttt{VOTA} (Verdict-Oriented Trace Abstraction): Three compressed components---exception events, assertion-point comparisons, and loop compression summaries.\n\\item \\texttt{exception\\_only}: Only exception and error events extracted from the trace (e.g., \\texttt{[ERROR] TypeError: ...}).\n\\item \\texttt{ae\\_only}: Only assertion-point actual/expected value pairs from the final comparison.\n\\item \\texttt{loop\\_only}: Only loop compression summaries (iteration counts and final variable states).\n\\item \\texttt{VOTA\\_no\\_ae}: VOTA without assertion-point information (exception events + loop compression).\n\\end{itemize}\n\nThese conditions span a spectrum from no trace information (\\texttt{no\\_trace}) through maximally compressed signals (\\texttt{exception\\_only}) to the complete unfiltered trace (\\texttt{full\\_trace}).\n\n\\begin{figure*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{fig1_trace_overview}\n\\caption{Overview of the verdict prediction pipeline. Source code and a test input are executed to produce a full raw trace. Four experimental conditions vary the trace information provided to the model: \\texttt{no\\_trace} (code only, 75.13\\%), \\texttt{full\\_trace} (complete trace, 81.76\\%), VOTA (compressed components, 99.41\\%), and \\texttt{exception\\_only} (exception events only, 99.83\\%). Each condition is paired with the same model to predict a pass/fail verdict. Accuracies shown are for Qwen-2.5-Coder-7B (seed=42, $N{=}1{,}190$).}\n\\label{fig:trace_overview}\n\\end{figure*}\n\n\\subsection{Models}\n\nWe evaluate two instruction-tuned code LLMs at the 7B parameter scale:\n\n\\begin{itemize}\n\\item \\textbf{Qwen-2.5-Coder-7B-Instruct} \\citep{qwen2025coder}: Pretrained on 5.5T tokens. Serves as the primary model for all ablation experiments.\n\\item \\textbf{DeepSeek-Coder-6.7B-Instruct} \\citep{deepseek2024coder}: Pretrained on 2T tokens. Used for cross-model validation to test whether findings generalize beyond a single model family.\n\\end{itemize}\n\nThe two models differ primarily in pretraining data volume (5.5T vs.\\ 2T tokens), providing a natural axis for evaluating whether trace signal utility is model-dependent \\citep{dong2024composition}.\n\n\\subsection{Training Setup}\n\nAll conditions use identical training configurations: LoRA \\citep{hu2022lora} (rank=16, $\\alpha$=32, dropout=0.05) on all linear layers, 3 epochs, effective batch size 32, learning rate $2 \\times 10^{-4}$ with linear warmup/decay, max sequence length 2{,}048 tokens.\nSequences exceeding this limit are truncated from the input while preserving the verdict label.\nThe primary seed is 42; seed robustness is evaluated with seeds 123 and 456 for \\texttt{exception\\_only}.\nFull hyperparameters are in Appendix~\\ref{sec:training_details}.\n\n\\subsection{Loss Ablation}\n\nTo disentangle training signal quality from input signal quality, we introduce a \\texttt{full\\_trace\\_label\\_only} variant.\nThis condition uses the same full trace as input but computes loss only on the verdict token, masking all trace tokens during training (loss weight = 0 on trace positions).\nIf this variant improves over standard \\texttt{full\\_trace}, the improvement is attributable to \\emph{gradient dilution}---trace tokens diluting the training signal without contributing to verdict prediction.\nThe remaining gap to VOTA is then attributable to \\emph{information overload}---irrelevant trace content that the model must process at inference time.",

      "totalLines": 64
    }

```text
1	\section{Related Work}
2	
3	\paragraph{Execution Traces for Code LLMs.}
4	Several recent efforts have incorporated execution traces into code language model training.
5	TracePile \citep{chen2025tracepile} scaled trace-augmented training to 2.6M samples across multiple programming languages, demonstrating gains on code generation tasks.
6	\citet{wang2025dcsh} conducted a comprehensive study of execution trace-based information for code LLMs across synthesis, repair, and reasoning tasks, finding that trace-based semantic information has limited and inconsistent utility---a finding our work extends by showing that for \emph{verdict prediction}, exception events are a clear dominant signal.
7	NExT \citep{ni2024next} used self-training on execution trace chain-of-thought rationales, improving program repair on MBPP by 26.1\%.
8	ET-CoT \citep{eguchi2025etcot} introduced sub-line granularity traces via PyTracify, achieving 70.0\% on CRUXEval-O with SFT on Qwen2.5-7B.
9	\citet{jung2025execution} used debugger-extracted traces as grounded supervision for LLM reasoning, reducing overthinking while improving cross-domain transfer.
10	\citet{ferrando2025execute} compared regular, compact, and dynamic scratchpad strategies, finding that compact traces (recording only changed variables) perform comparably to full traces---directly supporting our finding that complete value information is redundant.
11	CRUXEval \citep{gu2024cruxeval} established benchmarks for code execution reasoning; CRUXEval-X \citep{xu2025cruxevalx} extended this to 19 programming languages.
12	A common limitation across these studies is that they focus on generation or output prediction tasks; none have decomposed which trace components contribute to \emph{classification} accuracy or tested cross-model generalization of trace signals.
13	
14	\paragraph{Code Verdict and Patch Classification.}
15	TRACED \citep{ding2024traced} showed that execution-aware pretraining improves vulnerability detection and clone detection, using encoder-based models with trace-augmented objectives.
16	In automated patch correctness prediction, APPT \citep{appt2024} fine-tuned pre-trained models with BERT+LSTM achieving 79.7\% accuracy on Defects4J patches, while LLM4PatchCorrect \citep{zhou2024llm4patch} used Starcoder-7B zero-shot with execution traces reaching 84.4\%.
17	More recently, \citet{yang2025apsg} proposed Graph-LoRA with attributed patch semantic graphs for parameter-efficient patch classification.
18	FuseFL \citep{widyasari2024fusefl} fused SBFL rankings with test execution outcomes (including error messages) for fault localization, demonstrating that structured fault signals improve over raw inputs.
19	\citet{foster2025mutation} deployed LLM-based equivalence detection at scale at Meta, achieving 0.95 precision on mutation testing.
20	These approaches treat the trace (or patch) as a monolithic input; no prior work has isolated individual trace components to determine their relative contribution to classification, or evaluated whether trace-based classifiers generalize across model families.
21	
22	\paragraph{Trace Compression and Selection.}
23	TraceCoder \citep{huang2026tracecoder} inserts diagnostic probes at selected locations rather than recording full traces, improving Pass@1 by 34.4\% for LLM-generated code debugging.
24	InspectCoder \citep{wang2025inspectcoder} enables LLMs to actively control a debugger for strategic breakpoint placement and runtime state inspection, outperforming baselines by 5--60\%.
25	ND-Slicer \citep{yadavally2024ndslicer} uses execution-aware pretraining to predict dynamic program slices without execution, achieving 81.3\% exact match.
26	\citet{haque2025traces} found that raw execution traces provide limited benefit for program repair and that LLM-optimized prompt formats consistently outperform raw traces---supporting our finding that trace compression is essential.
27	\citet{wu2026runtime} used selective variable snapshots in a multi-agent repair debate framework, further validating that targeted trace extraction outperforms full recording.
28	These methods target repair tasks (generating fixes) rather than classification, and none analyze the information density trade-off between trace completeness and prediction accuracy.
29	Our information density decomposition---separating gradient dilution (26\%) from information overload (74\%)---provides the first quantitative framework for understanding \emph{why} trace compression helps, complementing these methods' focus on \emph{how} to compress.
30	
```

```text
1	\documentclass[11pt,a4paper]{article}
2	\usepackage[review]{acl}
3	% \usepackage{acl}  % uncomment for camera-ready (remove [review])
4	\usepackage{times,latexsym}
5	\usepackage{amsmath,amssymb}
6	\usepackage{graphicx}
7	\graphicspath{{../../figures/paper/}}
8	\usepackage{booktabs}
9	\usepackage{multirow}
10	\usepackage{xcolor}
11	
12	\title{Exception Events Are All You Need: \\Cross-Model Fault Detection for Verdict Prediction}
13	\author{Anonymous ACL submission}
14	
15	\begin{document}
16	\maketitle
17	
18	\begin{abstract}
19	Execution traces are increasingly used to enhance code language models, but which trace components matter for downstream tasks remains poorly understood.
20	We present the first systematic component ablation of trace representations for \emph{verdict prediction}---classifying whether a test passes or fails---across two models (Qwen-2.5-Coder-7B and DeepSeek-Coder-6.7B).
21	Our key finding is that exception events alone achieve near-perfect accuracy on Qwen (99.83\% $\pm$ 0.00, 3 seeds) and are the most effective component on DeepSeek (2/3 seeds at 99.33\%; 1 outlier at 91.09\% from LoRA initialization sensitivity), while a multi-component representation (VOTA) degrades by 14.96pp on the weaker model, confounded by training-time trace truncation.
22	We decompose the gap between full traces and compressed representations into gradient dilution (26\%) and information overload (74\%), establishing that irrelevant trace content---not training signal quality---is the primary bottleneck.
23	Component ablation reveals a two-tier diagnostic hierarchy: Tier~1 signals (exception events, assertion points) individually achieve ${\geq}$99.8\% accuracy, while Tier~2 signals (loop compression) reach only 85.6\%.
24	These results reframe verdict prediction as fault detection, where the minimal sufficient signal is a compact exception event that transfers well across model families; training stability is a secondary factor for smaller models.
25	\end{abstract}
26	
27	\input{introduction}
28	\input{method}
29	\input{experiments}
30	\input{discussion}
31	\input{related_work}
32	\input{conclusion}
33	
34	\section*{Limitations}
35	
36	Our study has several limitations.
37	First, our primary experiments use a single dataset (591 problems from HumanEval and MBPP-sanitized); while a cross-dataset evaluation on DebugBench (\S\ref{sec:debugbench}) confirms that exception dominance generalizes to synthetically injected bugs, generalization to other languages, larger codebases, or real-world fault distributions remains untested.
38	Second, we evaluate only two models at the 7B parameter scale; the relationship between model size and trace signal utility is unexplored.
39	Third, DeepSeek \texttt{exception\_only} exhibits seed-dependent variance: 2/3 seeds achieve 99.33\% while seed 123 yields 91.09\% ($\sigma{=}4.76$pp, $n{=}3$), attributable to LoRA initialization sensitivity (fail recall = 70.5\% for the outlier vs.\ 100\% for others).
40	Qwen shows perfect seed robustness ($\sigma{=}0$, $n{=}3$).
41	Whether training stabilization techniques can eliminate DeepSeek's seed sensitivity is unexplored; other conditions use a single seed.
42	Fourth, the initial DeepSeek \texttt{full\_trace} run was invalidated by context overflow; a re-run with extended context yields 79.16\% (CI [76.80\%, 81.30\%]), 2.60pp below Qwen (Table~\ref{tab:cross_model}).
43	Fifth, the candidate solutions were generated by Qwen-2.5-Coder-7B-Instruct, which also serves as one of our evaluation models; this does not affect verdict prediction but may influence the pass/fail distribution.
44	Finally, our analysis is limited to verdict prediction; whether exception dominance extends to other classification tasks or generation tasks is an open question.
45	
46	\section*{Ethics Statement}
47	
48	This work uses publicly available benchmarks (HumanEval, MBPP-sanitized, DebugBench) and does not involve human subjects, crowdsourced annotations, or personally identifiable information.
49	All models evaluated are open-source and publicly accessible.
50	Training uses parameter-efficient LoRA fine-tuning on two RTX PRO 6000 Blackwell GPUs, with each experiment using a single GPU and total compute under 20 GPU-hours across all experiments, resulting in minimal environmental impact.
51	We do not foresee direct negative societal impacts from this work; verdict prediction is a developer-facing tool for automated testing.
52	
53	\bibliography{references}
54	
55	\appendix
56	\input{appendix}
57	
58	\end{document}
59	
```

1	\section{Experiments}
2	
3	We evaluate across two research questions:
4	(1)~Do Tier~1 exception signals generalize across models?
5	(2)~What is the relative contribution of each trace component?
6	All experiments use $N{=}1{,}190$ test samples; accuracy is reported with Wilson 95\% CIs.
7	Pairwise comparisons use paired bootstrap resampling ($B{=}10{,}000$, seed=42); all differences are significant at $p{<}0.0001$ unless noted.
8	
9	\subsection{Cross-Model Comparison}
10	\label{sec:cross_model}
11	
12	Table~\ref{tab:cross_model} compares Qwen-2.5-7B and DeepSeek-6.7B under four conditions.
13	
14	\begin{table}[t]
15	\centering
16	\small
17	\begin{tabular}{lccc}
18	\toprule
19	\textbf{Condition} & \textbf{Qwen-2.5-7B} & \textbf{DeepSeek-6.7B} & \textbf{Gap} \\
20	\midrule
21	exception\_only & \textbf{99.83}\% & 99.33\%$^\dag$ & 0.50pp \\
22	VOTA & 99.41\% & 84.45\% & 14.96pp \\
23	VOTA\_no\_ae & 99.41\%$^\S$ & --- & --- \\
24	full\_trace & 81.76\% & 79.16\% & 2.60pp \\
25	no\_trace & 75.13\% & 73.36\% & 1.77pp \\
26	\midrule
27	Majority baseline & 71.51\% & 71.51\% & --- \\
28	\bottomrule
29	\end{tabular}
30	\caption{Cross-model accuracy ($N{=}1{,}190$). $^\dag$3-seed: 99.33\% (seeds 42, 456) and 91.09\% (seed 123, LoRA outlier). $^\S$Qwen only. Full CIs in Appendix~\ref{sec:full_results}.}
31	\label{tab:cross_model}
32	\end{table}
33	
34	Under \texttt{exception\_only}, Qwen achieves 99.83\% $\pm$ 0.00 across three seeds, while DeepSeek shows bimodal behavior: seeds 42 and 456 achieve 99.33\%, seed 123 yields 91.09\% (LoRA initialization outlier; fail recall = 70.5\%).
35	Even the outlier outperforms VOTA (84.45\%) by 6.64pp, confirming exception events as the most effective component across models.
36	The 14.96pp VOTA gap is confounded by tokenizer-driven truncation: 91.7\% of DeepSeek VOTA training samples exceed the 2{,}048-token context window, truncating diagnostic content (Appendix~\ref{sec:tokenizer_analysis}).
37	In contrast, \texttt{exception\_only} inputs average ${\sim}$200 tokens, making the cross-model comparison clean.
38	The \texttt{no\_trace} baseline confirms that source code alone provides limited signal, only marginally above majority-class guessing.
39	
40	\subsection{Component Ablation}
41	\label{sec:ablation}
42	
43	Table~\ref{tab:ablation} decomposes VOTA on Qwen-2.5-7B (seed=42), revealing a two-tier hierarchy.
44	
45	\begin{table}[t]
46	\centering
47	\small
48	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%
49	\begin{tabular}{llccc}
50	\toprule
51	\textbf{Condition} & \textbf{Components} & \textbf{Acc (\%)} & \textbf{95\% CI} & \textbf{Tier} \\
52	\midrule
53	ae\_only & assertion-point & \textbf{99.92} & [99.75, 100.00] & 1 \\
54	exception\_only & exception events & 99.83 & [99.58, 100.00] & 1 \\
55	VOTA & exc+ae+loop & 99.41 & [98.82, 99.83] & --- \\
56	VOTA\_no\_ae & exc+loop & 99.41 & [98.90, 99.80] & --- \\
57	loop\_only & loop compression & 85.63 & [83.53, 87.56] & 2 \\
58	full\_trace & complete trace & 81.76 & [79.70, 83.90] & --- \\
59	no\_trace & none & 75.13 & [72.77, 77.48] & --- \\
60	\midrule
61	Regex (best) & exc.\ type regex & 93.61 & --- & --- \\
62	\bottomrule
63	\end{tabular}%
64	}
65	\caption{Component ablation on Qwen-2.5-7B ($N{=}1{,}190$, seed=42). Tier~1 signals achieve near-perfect accuracy; combining them (VOTA) slightly dilutes performance.}
66	\label{tab:ablation}
67	\end{table}
68	
69	Tier~1 signals are individually sufficient: \texttt{ae\_only} (99.92\%) and \texttt{exception\_only} (99.83\%) are statistically indistinguishable ($p{=}0.80$) and both exceed VOTA ($p{=}0.01$), implying that combining signals introduces noise.
70	Removing assertion points has no effect (VOTA\_no\_ae = 99.41\%), confirming exception dominance.
71	Tier~2 \texttt{loop\_only} (85.63\%) improves 10.50pp over \texttt{no\_trace} ($p{<}0.0001$) but falls far short of Tier~1.
72	The strongest regex baseline (93.61\%) trails SFT by 6.22pp (McNemar $p{<}0.001$); the model learns to distinguish caught from uncaught exceptions (Appendix~\ref{sec:regex_baselines}).
73	
74	\subsection{Information Density Decomposition}
75	\label{sec:density}
76	
77	The 17.65pp gap between \texttt{full\_trace} (81.76\%) and VOTA (99.41\%) decomposes via \texttt{full\_trace\_label\_only}, which retains the full trace but masks all trace tokens from the loss.
78	
79	\begin{table}[t]
80	\centering
81	\small
82	\begin{tabular}{lccc}
83	\toprule
84	\textbf{Condition} & \textbf{Acc (\%)} & \textbf{$\Delta$ vs.\ full\_trace} & \textbf{Gap explained} \\
85	\midrule
86	full\_trace & 81.76 & --- & --- \\
87	label\_only & 86.22 & +4.46pp & 26\% \\
88	VOTA & 99.41 & +17.65pp & 100\% \\
89	\bottomrule
90	\end{tabular}
91	\caption{Information density decomposition: gradient dilution (26\%) vs.\ information overload (74\%).}
92	\label{tab:density}
93	\end{table}
94	
95	Gradient dilution accounts for 26\% (+4.46pp, $p{<}0.0001$, bootstrap 95\% CI $[{+}2.35, {+}6.55]$) and information overload for 74\% (+13.19pp).
96	
97	\subsection{Additional Analyses}
98	\label{sec:additional}
99	
100	\paragraph{Trace length ablation.}
101	\label{sec:trace_length}
102	Varying the trace token budget (256--no limit) on Qwen yields accuracy in a narrow 77.2\%--81.8\% band with a non-monotonic pattern, confirming that trace content quality---not quantity---is the bottleneck (Appendix~\ref{sec:trace_length_full}).
103	
104	\paragraph{Calibration.}
105	\label{sec:calibration}
106	Calibration mirrors the tier hierarchy: \texttt{exception\_only} ECE = 0.0027 vs.\ \texttt{no\_trace} ECE = 0.171---a 63$\times$ gap (Appendix~\ref{sec:calibration_full}).
107	
108	\paragraph{Cross-dataset generalization.}
109	\label{sec:debugbench}
110	On DebugBench \citep{tian2024debugbench} ($N{=}426$), the ranking is preserved: \texttt{exception\_only} 100\%, VOTA 99.77\%, \texttt{no\_trace} 96.71\%; narrow margins reflect a ceiling effect from structurally distinctive mutations (Appendix~\ref{sec:debugbench_full}).
111	
```

</details>

Full CIs in Appendix~\\ref{sec:full_results}.}\n\\label{tab:cross_model}\n\\end{table}\n\nUnder \\texttt{exception\\_only}, Qwen achieves 99.83\\% $\\pm$ 0.00 across three seeds, while DeepSeek shows bimodal behavior: seeds 42 and 456 achieve 99.33\\%, seed 123 yields 91.09\\% (LoRA initialization outlier; fail recall = 70.5\\%).\nEven the outlier outperforms VOTA (84.45\\%) by 6.64pp, confirming exception events as the most effective component across models.\nThe 14.96pp VOTA gap is confounded by tokenizer-driven truncation: 91.7\\% of DeepSeek VOTA training samples exceed the 2{,}048-token context window, truncating diagnostic content (Appendix~\\ref{sec:tokenizer_analysis}).\nIn contrast, \\texttt{exception\\_only} inputs average ${\\sim}$200 tokens, making the cross-model comparison clean.\nThe \\texttt{no\\_trace} baseline confirms that source code alone provides limited signal, only marginally above majority-class guessing.\n\n\\subsection{Component Ablation}\n\\label{sec:ablation}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:ablation} decomposes VOTA on Qwen-2.5-7B (seed=42), revealing a two-tier hierarchy.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{llccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Condition} & \\textbf{Components} & \\textbf{Acc (\\%)} & \\textbf{95\\% CI} & \\textbf{Tier} \\\\\n\\midrule\nae\\_only & assertion-point & \\textbf{99.92} & [99.75, 100.00] & 1 \\\\\nexception\\_only & exception events & 99.83 & [99.58, 100.00] & 1 \\\\\nVOTA & exc+ae+loop & 99.41 & [98.82, 99.83] & --- \\\\\nVOTA\\_no\\_ae & exc+loop & 99.41 & [98.90, 99.80] & --- \\\\\nloop\\_only & loop compression & 85.63 & [83.53, 87.56] & 2 \\\\\nfull\\_trace & complete trace & 81.76 & [79.70, 83.90] & --- \\\\\nno\\_trace & none & 75.13 & [72.77, 77.48] & --- \\\\\n\\midrule\nRegex (best) & exc.\\ type regex & 93.61 & --- & --- \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}%\n}\n\\caption{Component ablation on Qwen-2.5-7B ($N{=}1{,}190$, seed=42). Tier~1 signals achieve near-perfect accuracy; combining them (VOTA) slightly dilutes performance.}\n\\label{tab:ablation}\n\\end{table}\n\nTier~1 signals are individually sufficient: \\texttt{ae\\_only} (99.92\\%) and \\texttt{exception\\_only} (99.83\\%) are statistically indistinguishable ($p{=}0.80$) and both exceed VOTA ($p{=}0.01$), implying that combining signals introduces noise.\nRemoving assertion points has no effect (VOTA\\_no\\_ae = 99.41\\%), confirming exception dominance.\nTier~2 \\texttt{loop\\_only} (85.63\\%) improves 10.50pp over \\texttt{no\\_trace} ($p{<}0.0001$) but falls far short of Tier~1.\nThe strongest regex baseline (93.61\\%) trails SFT by 6.22pp (McNemar $p{<}0.001$); the model learns to distinguish caught from uncaught exceptions (Appendix~\\ref{sec:regex_baselines}).\n\n\\subsection{Information Density Decomposition}\n\\label{sec:density}\n\nThe 17.65pp gap between \\texttt{full\\_trace} (81.76\\%) and VOTA (99.41\\%) decomposes via \\texttt{full\\_trace\\_label\\_only}, which retains the full trace but masks all trace tokens from the loss.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Condition} & \\textbf{Acc (\\%)} & \\textbf{$\\Delta$ vs.\\ full\\_trace} & \\textbf{Gap explained} \\\\\n\\midrule\nfull\\_trace & 81.76 & --- & --- \\\\\nlabel\\_only & 86.22 & +4.46pp & 26\\% \\\\\nVOTA & 99.41 & +17.65pp & 100\\% \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Information density decomposition: gradient dilution (26\\%) vs.\\ information overload (74\\%).}\n\\label{tab:density}\n\\end{table}\n\nGradient dilution accounts for 26\\% (+4.46pp, $p{<}0.0001$, bootstrap 95\\% CI $[{+}2.35, {+}6.55]$) and information overload for 74\\% (+13.19pp).\n\n\\subsection{Additional Analyses}\n\\label{sec:additional}\n\n\\paragraph{Trace length ablation.}\n\\label{sec:trace_length}\nVarying the trace token budget (256--no limit) on Qwen yields accuracy in a narrow 77.2\\%--81.8\\% band with a non-monotonic pattern, confirming that trace content quality---not quantity---is the bottleneck (Appendix~\\ref{sec:trace_length_full}).\n\n\\paragraph{Calibration.}\n\\label{sec:calibration}\nCalibration mirrors the tier hierarchy: \\texttt{exception\\_only} ECE = 0.0027 vs.\\ \\texttt{no\\_trace} ECE = 0.171---a 63$\\times$ gap (Appendix~\\ref{sec:calibration_full}).\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-dataset generalization.}\n\\label{sec:debugbench}\nOn DebugBench \\citep{tian2024debugbench} ($N{=}426$), the ranking is preserved: \\texttt{exception\\_only} 100\\%, VOTA 99.77\\%, \\texttt{no\\_trace} 96.71\\%; narrow margins reflect a ceiling effect from structurally distinctive mutations (Appendix~\\ref{sec:debugbench_full}).\n",
    "structuredPatch": [

    "newString": "\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Information density decomposition: gradient dilution (26\\%) vs.\\ information overload (74\\%).}\n\\label{tab:density}\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Condition} & \\textbf{Acc (\\%)} & \\textbf{$\\Delta$ vs.\\ full\\_trace} & \\textbf{Gap explained} \\\\\n\\midrule\nfull\\_trace & 81.76 & --- & --- \\\\\nlabel\\_only & 86.22 & +4.46pp & 26\\% \\\\\nVOTA & 99.41 & +17.65pp & 100\\% \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Experiments}\n\nWe evaluate across two research questions:\n(1)~Do Tier~1 exception signals generalize across models?\n(2)~What is the relative contribution of each trace component?\nAll experiments use $N{=}1{,}190$ test samples; accuracy is reported with Wilson 95\\% CIs.\nPairwise comparisons use paired bootstrap resampling ($B{=}10{,}000$, seed=42); all differences are significant at $p{<}0.0001$ unless noted.\n\n\\subsection{Cross-Model Comparison}\n\\label{sec:cross_model}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:cross_model} compares Qwen-2.5-7B and DeepSeek-6.7B under four conditions.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Cross-model accuracy ($N{=}1{,}190$). $^\\dag$3-seed: 99.33\\% (seeds 42, 456) and 91.09\\% (seed 123, LoRA outlier). $^\\S$Qwen only. Full CIs in Appendix~\\ref{sec:full_results}.}\n\\label{tab:cross_model}\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Condition} & \\textbf{Qwen-2.5-7B} & \\textbf{DeepSeek-6.7B} & \\textbf{Gap} \\\\\n\\midrule\nexception\\_only & \\textbf{99.83}\\% & 99.33\\%$^\\dag$ & 0.50pp \\\\\nVOTA & 99.41\\% & 84.45\\% & 14.96pp \\\\\nVOTA\\_no\\_ae & 99.41\\%$^\\S$ & --- & --- \\\\\nfull\\_trace & 81.76\\% & 79.16\\% & 2.60pp \\\\\nno\\_trace & 75.13\\% & 73.36\\% & 1.77pp \\\\\n\\midrule\nMajority baseline & 71.51\\% & 71.51\\% & --- \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nUnder \\texttt{exception\\_only}, Qwen achieves 99.83\\% $\\pm$ 0.00 across three seeds, while DeepSeek shows bimodal behavior: seeds 42 and 456 achieve 99.33\\%, seed 123 yields 91.09\\% (LoRA initialization outlier; fail recall = 70.5\\%).\nEven the outlier outperforms VOTA (84.45\\%) by 6.64pp, confirming exception events as the most effective component across models.\nThe 14.96pp VOTA gap is confounded by tokenizer-driven truncation: 91.7\\% of DeepSeek VOTA training samples exceed the 2{,}048-token context window, truncating diagnostic content (Appendix~\\ref{sec:tokenizer_analysis}).\nIn contrast, \\texttt{exception\\_only} inputs average ${\\sim}$200 tokens, making the cross-model comparison clean.\nThe \\texttt{no\\_trace} baseline confirms that source code alone provides limited signal, only marginally above majority-class guessing.\n\n\\subsection{Component Ablation}\n\\label{sec:ablation}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:ablation} decomposes VOTA on Qwen-2.5-7B (seed=42), revealing a two-tier hierarchy.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Component ablation on Qwen-2.5-7B ($N{=}1{,}190$, seed=42). Tier~1 signals achieve near-perfect accuracy; combining them (VOTA) slightly dilutes performance.}\n\\label{tab:ablation}\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{llccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Condition} & \\textbf{Components} & \\textbf{Acc (\\%)} & \\textbf{95\\% CI} & \\textbf{Tier} \\\\\n\\midrule\nae\\_only & assertion-point & \\textbf{99.92} & [99.75, 100.00] & 1 \\\\\nexception\\_only & exception events & 99.83 & [99.58, 100.00] & 1 \\\\\nVOTA & exc+ae+loop & 99.41 & [98.82, 99.83] & --- \\\\\nVOTA\\_no\\_ae & exc+loop & 99.41 & [98.90, 99.80] & --- \\\\\nloop\\_only & loop compression & 85.63 & [83.53, 87.56] & 2 \\\\\nfull\\_trace & complete trace & 81.76 & [79.70, 83.90] & --- \\\\\nno\\_trace & none & 75.13 & [72.77, 77.48] & --- \\\\\n\\midrule\nRegex (best) & exc.\\ type regex & 93.61 & --- & --- \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}%\n}\n\\end{table}\n\nTier~1 signals are individually sufficient: \\texttt{ae\\_only} (99.92\\%) and \\texttt{exception\\_only} (99.83\\%) are statistically indistinguishable ($p{=}0.80$) and both exceed VOTA ($p{=}0.01$), implying that combining signals introduces noise.\nRemoving assertion points has no effect (VOTA\\_no\\_ae = 99.41\\%), confirming exception dominance.\nTier~2 \\texttt{loop\\_only} (85.63\\%) improves 10.50pp over \\texttt{no\\_trace} ($p{<}0.0001$) but falls far short of Tier~1.\nThe strongest regex baseline (93.61\\%) trails SFT by 6.22pp (McNemar $p{<}0.001$); the model learns to distinguish caught from uncaught exceptions (Appendix~\\ref{sec:regex_baselines}).\n\n\\subsection{Information Density Decomposition}\n\\label{sec:density}\n\nThe 17.65pp gap between \\texttt{full\\_trace} (81.76\\%) and VOTA (99.41\\%) decomposes via \\texttt{full\\_trace\\_label\\_only}, which retains the full trace but masks all trace tokens from the loss.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Condition} & \\textbf{Acc (\\%)} & \\textbf{$\\Delta$ vs.\\ full\\_trace} & \\textbf{Gap explained} \\\\\n\\midrule\nfull\\_trace & 81.76 & --- & --- \\\\\nlabel\\_only & 86.22 & +4.46pp & 26\\% \\\\\nVOTA & 99.41 & +17.65pp & 100\\% \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Information density decomposition: gradient dilution (26\\%) vs.\\ information overload (74\\%).}\n\\label{tab:density}\n\\end{table}\n\nGradient dilution accounts for 26\\% (+4.46pp, $p{<}0.0001$, bootstrap 95\\% CI $[{+}2.35, {+}6.55]$) and information overload for 74\\% (+13.19pp).\n\n\\subsection{Additional Analyses}\n\\label{sec:additional}\n\n\\paragraph{Trace length ablation.}\n\\label{sec:trace_length}\nVarying the trace token budget (256--no limit) on Qwen yields accuracy in a narrow 77.2\\%--81.8\\% band with a non-monotonic pattern, confirming that trace content quality---not quantity---is the bottleneck (Appendix~\\ref{sec:trace_length_full}).\n\n\\paragraph{Calibration.}\n\\label{sec:calibration}\nCalibration mirrors the tier hierarchy: \\texttt{exception\\_only} ECE = 0.0027 vs.\\ \\texttt{no\\_trace} ECE = 0.171---a 63$\\times$ gap (Appendix~\\ref{sec:calibration_full}).\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-dataset generalization.}\n\\label{sec:debugbench}\nOn DebugBench \\citep{tian2024debugbench} ($N{=}426$), the ranking is preserved: \\texttt{exception\\_only} 100\\%, VOTA 99.77\\%, \\texttt{no\\_trace} 96.71\\%; narrow margins reflect a ceiling effect from structurally distinctive mutations (Appendix~\\ref{sec:debugbench_full}).\n",

102	\section{VOTA Trace Examples}
103	\label{sec:vota_examples}
104	
105	We provide two representative VOTA trace examples illustrating the fail and pass cases.
106	In the fail case, the exception event provides an unambiguous fault signal.
107	In the pass case, the absence of an exception event combined with a matching assertion point confirms correct execution.
108	
109	\paragraph{Fail example.}
110	A \texttt{ZeroDivisionError} triggers an \texttt{[ERROR]} event, providing a direct fault signal that is sufficient for the model to predict \texttt{fail}.
111	
112	\begin{small}
113	\begin{verbatim}
114	## Function
115	def divide(a, b):
116	    return a / b
117	
118	## Test Input
119	divide(1, 0)
120	
121	## Expected Output
122	inf
123	
124	## VOTA Trace
125	[ERROR] ZeroDivisionError: division by zero
126	
127	## Verdict
128	fail
129	\end{verbatim}
130	\end{small}
131	
132	\paragraph{Pass example.}
133	No exception is raised, and the assertion point confirms that the actual output matches the expected value.
134	
135	\begin{small}
136	\begin{verbatim}
137	## Function
138	def add(a, b):
139	    return a + b
140	
141	## Test Input
142	add(2, 3)
143	
144	## Expected Output
145	5
146	
147	## VOTA Trace
148	[LOOP] No loops detected.
149	[ASSERT] actual=5, expected=5
150	
151	## Verdict
152	pass
153	\end{verbatim}
154	\end{small}
155	
156	These examples illustrate the core asymmetry exploited by the \texttt{exception\_only} condition: fail cases produce an explicit \texttt{[ERROR]} event, while pass cases are characterized by its absence.
157	This binary signal---presence vs.\ absence of an exception---is sufficient for near-perfect verdict prediction.
158

211	\section{Tokenizer and Truncation Analysis}
212	\label{sec:tokenizer_analysis}
213	
214	The VOTA cross-model gap (14.96pp, Table~\ref{tab:cross_model}) is confounded by a training-time truncation asymmetry.
215	The DeepSeek tokenizer averages 2{,}626 tokens per VOTA sample versus 1{,}929 for Qwen, causing 91.7\% of DeepSeek VOTA training samples to exceed the 2{,}048-token context window.
216	The training framework preserves the verdict label but truncates trace content, so the model trains with incomplete diagnostic information for the majority of samples.
217	
218	A post-hoc group analysis provides directional evidence: partitioning DeepSeek VOTA test samples by whether their training-set counterparts exceeded the context window, intact samples ($N{=}99$) achieve 100\% accuracy while truncated samples ($N{=}1{,}091$) achieve 83.04\% (95\% CI [80.75\%, 85.24\%]; Fisher exact $p{<}0.001$), with all 185 errors concentrated in the fail class (fail recall = 31.23\%).
219	Per-class analysis (Table~\ref{tab:per_class}) confirms that DeepSeek VOTA achieves 100\% fail precision but only 45.43\% fail recall, systematically defaulting to \texttt{pass}---in contrast to Qwen VOTA, which achieves 100\% fail recall.
220	
221	Seed robustness diverges across models: Qwen \texttt{exception\_only} produces identical 99.83\% across all three seeds ($\sigma{=}0$), while DeepSeek shows bimodal behavior: seeds 42 and 456 achieve 99.33\% (fail recall = 100\%), seed 123 yields 91.09\% (fail recall = 70.5\%).
222	Because \texttt{exception\_only} test inputs contain 48.99\% duplicates (607 unique of 1,190; \S\ref{sec:dataset}), CIs assume $n{=}1{,}190$ independent trials; under deduplication ($n{=}607$), Qwen's accuracy is 99.67\% (Wilson CI [98.81, 99.91]).
223	
224	%% ============================================================
225	\section{Irreducible Sample Analysis}
226	\label{sec:irreducible}
227	
228	The \texttt{exception\_only} condition with Qwen-2.5-Coder-7B misclassifies exactly 2 out of 1,190 test samples across all three seeds (42, 123, 456), with 100\% overlap across seeds.
229	Both samples (IDs 901 and 905) share the following characteristics:
230	
231	\begin{itemize}
232	\item \textbf{Function:} \texttt{check\_dict\_case} (HumanEval/95)---a function that checks whether all dictionary keys are uniformly upper-case or lower-case strings.
233	\item \textbf{Ground-truth label:} \texttt{pass}.
234	\item \textbf{Predicted label:} \texttt{fail} (false negative).
235	\item \textbf{Cross-seed consistency:} Both samples are misclassified identically by all three seeds, indicating a systematic rather than stochastic error.
236	\end{itemize}
237	
238	Manual inspection reveals the root cause: \texttt{check\_dict\_case} uses \texttt{all()} over a generator expression, and Python's \texttt{all()} short-circuits on the first \texttt{True} value, raising \texttt{GeneratorExit} to terminate the generator.
239	Because \texttt{GeneratorExit} is a \texttt{BaseException} subclass, the tracer records it as seven \texttt{[ERROR] GeneratorExit} lines---even though the function executes correctly and the test passes.
240	The model observes these spurious error markers and reasonably but incorrectly predicts \texttt{fail}.
241	This is not a model failure but a representation ceiling: the \texttt{exception\_only} format cannot distinguish genuine exceptions from benign \texttt{BaseException} subclasses triggered by Python's iterator protocol.
242	
243	These 2 irreducible errors establish a practical accuracy ceiling of $1 - 2/1190 = 99.83\%$ for the \texttt{exception\_only} condition on this dataset, suggesting that further improvements would require either supplementary trace signals or modifications to the input representation.
244	
245	%% ============================================================
246	\section{Trace Length Ablation}
247	\label{sec:trace_length_full}
248	
249	To test whether the \texttt{full\_trace} performance gap is caused by input truncation at training time, we vary the maximum trace token budget on Qwen-2.5-7B (seed=42) while keeping all other hyperparameters fixed.
250

Accuracy remains in a narrow band regardless of trace length, confirming that trace content quality---not quantity---is the bottleneck.}

274	
275	As shown in Table~\ref{tab:trace_length} and Figure~\ref{fig:trace_length}, all four trace length conditions fall within a 5pp range (77.2\%--81.8\%).
276	To quantify the reliability of the observed differences, Table~\ref{tab:trace_length_pairwise} reports paired bootstrap comparisons ($B{=}10{,}000$, seed=42) across all condition pairs.
277

295	& 1024 $-$ no\_trace      & $+$2.10  & [$-$0.17, $+$4.37]   & 0.078 \\
296	& no\_limit $-$ no\_trace & $+$6.64  & [$+$4.37, $+$8.99]   & ${<}$0.001*** \\
297	\midrule
298	\multirow{4}{*}{\rotatebox{90}{\scriptsize vs.\ exc.}}

300	& 512 $-$ exc\_only       & $-$19.41 & [$-$21.76, $-$17.14] & ${<}$0.001*** \\
301	& 1024 $-$ exc\_only      & $-$22.61 & [$-$25.04, $-$20.25] & ${<}$0.001*** \\

306	\caption{Pairwise paired bootstrap comparisons for trace length ablation ($B{=}10{,}000$, seed=42). $\Delta$ = accuracy difference between the two conditions (first $-$ second in the budget group; condition $-$ baseline in the other groups). **$p{<}0.01$; ***$p{<}0.001$.}
307	\label{tab:trace_length_pairwise}
308	\end{table}
309	
310	\paragraph{Non-monotonic pattern.}
311	The trend across trace budgets is non-monotonic: 512 tokens (80.42\%) slightly outperforms 256 tokens (78.82\%), but doubling to 1024 tokens \emph{decreases} accuracy to 77.23\%---a statistically significant 3.19pp drop ($p{=}0.002$, 95\% CI [$-$5.29, $-$1.09]).
312	The no-limit condition (81.76\%) recovers and significantly exceeds 1024 tokens ($p{<}0.001$), but 1024 tokens is not significantly better than the \texttt{no\_trace} baseline (75.13\%; $\Delta{=}{+}2.10$pp, $p{=}0.078$, 95\% CI [$-$0.17, $+$4.37]).
313	This means that truncating traces to an intermediate length can be \emph{worse} than providing no trace at all---the 1024-token budget retains enough irrelevant content to mislead the model while discarding diagnostic events that might appear late in the trace.
314	
315	\paragraph{Comparison to baselines.}
316	All four trace budget conditions significantly exceed \texttt{no\_trace} except 1024 tokens ($p{=}0.078$), confirming that raw trace content provides some signal but unreliably so.
317	The gap to \texttt{exception\_only} (99.83\%) is massive for every budget condition (18--23pp, all $p{<}0.001$), underscoring that even unlimited raw traces cannot substitute for targeted signal extraction.
318	The best full-trace condition (no\_limit, 81.76\%) still lags \texttt{exception\_only} by 18.07pp (95\% CI [$-$20.34, $-$15.88]).
319	
320	\paragraph{Interpretation.}
321	These results rule out input truncation as the primary explanation for the full-trace performance gap: even with unlimited tokens, the model cannot extract the diagnostic signal that VOTA's targeted compression provides.
322	The non-monotonic dip at 1024 tokens is consistent with a ``worst-of-both-worlds'' regime where the model receives enough noisy context to dilute the signal but not enough to compensate through broader coverage.
323	The result reinforces the information density interpretation (\S\ref{sec:density}): the gap between full traces and VOTA is driven by the \emph{content} of the trace (irrelevant lines diluting the diagnostic signal), not its \emph{length}.
324	In contrast, VOTA's compressed representation concentrates the diagnostic signal into fewer tokens, making it sensitive to truncation when those tokens are removed (\S\ref{sec:cross_model}).
325

327	\section{Calibration Analysis}
328	\label{sec:calibration_full}
329	
330	Beyond accuracy, we evaluate whether trace conditions affect prediction calibration, motivated by recent findings that code LLM confidence is often poorly calibrated \citep{spiess2025calibration}.
331	
332	\begin{table}[t]
333	\centering
334	\small
335	\begin{tabular}{lcc}
336	\toprule
337	\textbf{Condition} & \textbf{ECE $\downarrow$} & \textbf{Brier $\downarrow$} \\
338	\midrule
339	ae\_only & \textbf{0.0004} & \textbf{0.0008} \\
340	exception\_only & 0.0027 & 0.0013 \\
341	VOTA & 0.0054 & 0.005 \\
342	full\_trace & 0.096 & 0.144 \\
343	no\_trace & 0.171 & 0.197 \\
344	\bottomrule
345	\end{tabular}
346	\caption{Calibration metrics on Qwen-2.5-7B ($N{=}1{,}190$, seed=42). ECE = expected calibration error; Brier = Brier score. Lower is better for both.}
347	\label{tab:calibration}
348	\end{table}
349	
350	Table~\ref{tab:calibration} shows that calibration quality mirrors the Tier~1/2 hierarchy.
351	Tier~1 conditions (\texttt{ae\_only}: ECE=0.0004; \texttt{exception\_only}: ECE=0.0027) produce near-perfectly calibrated predictions, while \texttt{full\_trace} (ECE=0.096) and \texttt{no\_trace} (ECE=0.171) exhibit substantial miscalibration.
352	VOTA (ECE=0.0054) is well-calibrated but slightly worse than either Tier~1 component alone, reinforcing the pattern that combining trace signals dilutes rather than improves the prediction signal.
353	
354	The Brier scores tell a consistent story: \texttt{ae\_only} (0.0008) and \texttt{exception\_only} (0.0013) achieve near-zero scores, while \texttt{no\_trace} (0.197) indicates substantial miscalibration.
355

360	\label{fig:calibration}

363	%% ============================================================
364	\section{Cross-Dataset Generalization: DebugBench}
365	\label{sec:debugbench_full}
366	
367	To test whether exception-based verdict prediction generalizes beyond LLM-generated solutions, we evaluate on DebugBench \citep{tian2024debugbench}---a dataset of human-written programs with synthetically injected bugs (single-point mutations such as operator replacement, variable misuse, and missing statements).
368	After filtering for executable Python samples with valid traces, we retain 426 samples with a near-balanced class distribution (majority baseline: 51.4\%).
369	
370	\begin{table}[t]
371	\centering
372	\small
373	\begin{tabular}{lcc}
374	\toprule
375	\textbf{Condition} & \textbf{Acc (\%)} & \textbf{95\% CI} \\
376	\midrule
377	exception\_only & \textbf{100.00} & [99.14, 100.00] \\
378	VOTA & 99.77 & [99.30, 100.00] \\
379	no\_trace & 96.71 & [94.80, 98.40] \\
380	\midrule
381	Majority baseline & 51.41 & --- \\
382	\bottomrule
383	\end{tabular}
384	\caption{Generalization to DebugBench ($N{=}426$, Qwen-2.5-7B, seed=42). Wilson 95\% CIs. The component ranking (exception\_only $\geq$ VOTA $>$ no\_trace) is preserved, and exception\_only achieves perfect accuracy.}
385	\label{tab:debugbench}
386	\end{table}
387	
388	Table~\ref{tab:debugbench} shows that the component ranking from the main experiments is preserved: \texttt{exception\_only} (100.00\%) $\geq$ VOTA (99.77\%) $>$ \texttt{no\_trace} (96.71\%).
389	The \texttt{exception\_only} condition achieves perfect accuracy, confirming that the exception-based representation generalizes to a different fault distribution without degradation.
390	
391	The absolute margins are much narrower than on the main dataset: the \texttt{no\_trace} baseline already achieves 96.71\%---compared to 75.13\% on HumanEval+MBPP---leaving only 3.29pp of headroom for trace-based improvement.
392	This ceiling effect reflects a fundamental difference in fault characteristics: DebugBench bugs are synthetically injected single-point mutations, which produce code that is structurally dissimilar from the original correct solution; a model can often identify the fault from source code alone.
393	In contrast, the HumanEval+MBPP solutions are LLM-generated, producing natural errors (logic mistakes, edge-case failures) where the faulty and correct solutions may be structurally similar, making code-only verdict prediction substantially harder.
394	
395	These results support exception-based verdict prediction as a generalizable approach---the method does not degrade on an out-of-distribution dataset, and the exception dominance ranking (exception\_only $\geq$ VOTA $>$ no\_trace) is consistent across both datasets---but the narrow DebugBench margins are insufficient to independently validate trace importance.
396	The 24.70pp trace lift on the main dataset (no\_trace 75.13\% $\to$ exception\_only 99.83\%) remains the primary evidence for the value of exception-based trace signals.
397	
398	%% ============================================================
399	\section{Cross-Model Robustness Analysis}
400	\label{sec:robustness_analysis}
401	
402	The cross-model gap pattern (Table~\ref{tab:cross_model}) suggests a design principle: \emph{trace representations should match the model capability floor, not the ceiling.}
403	Exception events are consistently the most effective component on both models, and their structural simplicity---a fixed-format error message---ensures that the relative advantage over richer representations holds across all seeds.
404	Two of three DeepSeek seeds match Qwen-level accuracy (99.33\%); the outlier (seed 123, 91.09\%) is attributable to LoRA initialization landing in a suboptimal basin (fail recall = 70.5\%), not to a failure of the exception signal itself---even this outlier outperforms VOTA (84.45\%) by 6.64pp.
405	The VOTA cross-model gap is confounded by two factors: (1)~tokenizer efficiency---the DeepSeek tokenizer averages 2{,}626 tokens per VOTA sample versus Qwen's 1{,}929, causing 91.7\% of training samples to exceed the 2{,}048-token context window; content-aware truncation preferentially removes the terminal structured prompt, preserving the verdict label but truncating trace content---and (2)~base model capability differences, consistent with findings that SFT generalization depends on pretrain quality \citep{yang2024generalization}.
406	The two factors cannot be disentangled in the current experimental design.
407	Our \texttt{exception\_only} design sidesteps the truncation confound entirely: inputs average ${\sim}$200 tokens, well below the 2{,}048-token limit, so no training samples are truncated on either model.
408	The resulting 0.50pp cross-model gap (Table~\ref{tab:cross_model}) is therefore a clean measure of model capability differences, making the ``capability floor'' principle directly testable.
409	The practical implication is that trace-based systems should default to the simplest sufficient signal; for smaller models, training stability (multi-seed selection, warmup schedules) is a secondary but addressable concern.
410	
411	%% ============================================================
412	\section{Training Dynamics and Loss Decoupling}
413	\label{sec:training_dynamics}
414	
415	The DeepSeek \texttt{exception\_only} results reveal two instructive training dynamics.
416	First, epoch-level analysis shows mild degradation in 2/3 seeds: seed 42 peaks at 99.83\% after epoch~1 but settles at 99.33\% by epoch~3 ($-$0.50pp), seed 123 degrades from 91.43\% to 91.09\% ($-$0.34pp), while seed 456 shows no epoch degradation (99.33\% at both epoch~1 and epoch~3).
417	Early stopping may yield marginal gains for some seeds, but the epoch effect is small (${\leq}$0.5pp) compared to the seed effect (8.24pp between seeds 42 and 123).
418	
419	Second, seeds 123 and 456 converge to nearly identical final training losses (0.122 and 0.123 respectively), yet their accuracies differ by 8.24pp (91.09\% vs.\ 99.33\%).
420	The critical difference lies in fail recall: seeds 42 and 456 achieve 100\%, while seed 123 achieves only 70.5\%---the LoRA initialization in seed 123 fails to learn the exception$\to$fail mapping for a substantial fraction of failing samples, even though the per-token loss appears converged.
421	This demonstrates that token-level training loss can mask fundamental differences in sample-level classification ability, suggesting a parallel to the eval-loss/accuracy decoupling observed in DeepSeek VOTA (where eval loss rose 65\% from epoch 1 to 3 while accuracy improved by 6.3pp) at the training phase.
422	The practical recommendation is twofold: (1)~use task-specific metrics (accuracy, fail recall) for checkpoint and seed selection rather than training or evaluation loss, and (2)~for deployment on smaller models, train multiple seeds and select the best, as a single run may not reliably converge to the correct classification boundary.
423	
424	The contrast with VOTA is instructive: DeepSeek VOTA accuracy monotonically increases from 78.15\% to 84.45\% across epochs, but is consistent with a performance limit associated with the high trace truncation rate (\S\ref{sec:cross_model})---the model progressively extracts signal from samples with intact trace content but cannot fully compensate for the information loss in the truncated majority.
425	The 84.45\% accuracy reflects a combination of trace information loss and model capability constraints.
426	
427	%% ============================================================
428	\section{Deduplication Statistics}
429	\label{sec:dedup_stats}
430	
431	Table~\ref{tab:dedup} reports the duplication rates across trace formats.
432	Because multiple passing solutions to the same problem produce identical (input, output) pairs in the \texttt{exception\_only} format---correct executions raise no exceptions, so the trace content is the same regardless of the solution's internal logic---the dataset contains duplicate rows.
433	Train--test input overlap is zero (split by problem ID), so no information leakage occurs.
434	The high duplication in \texttt{exception\_only} does not affect our experimental conclusions: after deduplication the training set still contains 2,858 unique samples and the test set 607, providing sufficient coverage for the 591-problem benchmark.
435	The \texttt{exception\_only} test set contains 48.99\% duplicate inputs due to representation compactness; all confidence intervals treat samples as independent Bernoulli trials.
436	
437	\begin{table}[t]
438	\centering
439	\small
440	\begin{tabular}{lccc}
441	\toprule
442	\textbf{Split} & \textbf{Total} & \textbf{Unique} & \textbf{Dup.\ rate} \\
443	\midrule
444	exception\_only train & 4{,}720 & 2{,}858 & 39.45\% \\
445	exception\_only test  & 1{,}190 &   607 & 48.99\% \\
446	raw\_traces (full)    & 5{,}910 & 5{,}803 &  1.81\% \\
447	\bottomrule
448	\end{tabular}
449	\caption{Deduplication statistics. The \texttt{exception\_only} format collapses multiple passing solutions into identical rows because correct executions produce no exception events. Train--test input overlap is 0 (no leakage).}
450	\label{tab:dedup}
451	\end{table}
452	
453	%% ============================================================
454	\section{Regex Baseline Analysis}
455	\label{sec:regex_baselines}
456	
457	To establish a non-learning upper bound, we evaluate five regex-based baselines on the \texttt{exception\_only} test set ($N{=}1{,}190$) and two format-specific baselines on VOTA and \texttt{full\_trace} inputs.
458	
459	\begin{table}[t]
460	\centering
461	\small
462	\begin{tabular}{llc}
463	\toprule
464	\textbf{Baseline} & \textbf{Method} & \textbf{Acc (\%)} \\
465	\midrule
466	B1: \texttt{[ERROR]} presence & string match & 93.45 \\
467	B2: Traceback presence & string match & 71.51 \\
468	B3: Any keyword & union of B1+B2+Exception & 93.45 \\
469	B4: Exception type regex & regex on exception class names & \textbf{93.61} \\
470	B5: Format marker & \texttt{[No exception data]} $\to$ pass & 99.33$^\ddag$ \\
471	\midrule
472	VOTA format regex & best regex on VOTA traces & 75.55 \\
473	full\_trace format regex & best regex on full traces & 77.39 \\
474	\bottomrule
475	\end{tabular}
476	\caption{Regex baseline accuracy ($N{=}1{,}190$). B4 is the strongest legitimate baseline (6.22pp below SFT \texttt{exception\_only}, McNemar $p{<}0.001$). $^\ddag$B5 exploits a data formatting artifact: the \texttt{[No exception data in trace]} marker is deterministically present in all pass samples, making this a format-level shortcut rather than a genuine exception-based heuristic.}
477	\label{tab:regex}
478	\end{table}
479	
480	The strongest legitimate baseline (B4, 93.61\%) applies regex pattern-matching on Python exception class names (e.g., \texttt{ValueError}, \texttt{TypeError}).
481	Its errors concentrate on 8 boundary samples where a \texttt{ValueError} is raised inside a \texttt{try/except} block and caught---the program passes despite an exception occurring in the trace.
482	SFT \texttt{exception\_only} correctly classifies 6 of these 8 samples, indicating that the model learns the semantic distinction between caught (benign) and uncaught (fault-indicating) exceptions.
483	B5 (99.33\%) achieves near-SFT accuracy but relies on the deterministic presence of a format marker in pass-class traces, which is an artifact of the trace preprocessing pipeline rather than a generalizable signal; McNemar test vs.\ SFT yields $p{=}0.041$, confirming a statistically significant difference.
484

2{,}626 tokens per VOTA sample versus 1{,}929 for Qwen, causing 91.7\\% of DeepSeek VOTA training samples to exceed the 2{,}048-token context window.\nThe training framework preserves the verdict label but truncates trace content, so the model trains with incomplete diagnostic information for the majority of samples.\n\nA post-hoc group analysis provides directional evidence: partitioning DeepSeek VOTA test samples by whether their training-set counterparts exceeded the context window, intact samples ($N{=}99$) achieve 100\\% accuracy while truncated samples ($N{=}1{,}091$) achieve 83.04\\% (95\\% CI [80.75\\%, 85.24\\%]; Fisher exact $p{<}0.001$), with all 185 errors concentrated in the fail class (fail recall = 31.23\\%).\nPer-class analysis (Table~\\ref{tab:per_class}) confirms that DeepSeek VOTA achieves 100\\% fail precision but only 45.43\\% fail recall, systematically defaulting to \\texttt{pass}---in contrast to Qwen VOTA, which achieves 100\\% fail recall.\n\nSeed robustness diverges across models: Qwen \\texttt{exception\\_only} produces identical 99.83\\% across all three seeds ($\\sigma{=}0$), while DeepSeek shows bimodal behavior: seeds 42 and 456 achieve 99.33\\% (fail recall = 100\\%), seed 123 yields 91.09\\% (fail recall = 70.5\\%).\nBecause \\texttt{exception\\_only} test inputs contain 48.99\\% duplicates (607 unique of 1,190; ============================================================\n\\section{Irreducible Sample Analysis}\n\\label{sec:irreducible}\n\nThe \\texttt{exception\\_only} condition with Qwen-2.5-Coder-7B misclassifies exactly 2 out of 1,190 test samples across all three seeds (42, 123, 456), with 100\\% overlap across seeds.\nBoth samples (IDs 901 and 905) share the following characteristics:\n\n\\begin{itemize}\n\\item \\textbf{Function:} \\texttt{check\\_dict\\_case} (HumanEval/95)---a function that checks whether all dictionary keys are uniformly upper-case or lower-case strings.\n\\item \\textbf{Ground-truth label:} \\texttt{pass}.\n\\item \\textbf{Predicted label:} \\texttt{fail} (false negative).\n\\item \\textbf{Cross-seed consistency:} Both samples are misclassified identically by all three seeds, indicating a systematic rather than stochastic error.\n\\end{itemize}\n\nManual inspection reveals the root cause: \\texttt{check\\_dict\\_case} uses \\texttt{all()} over a generator expression, and Python's \\texttt{all()} short-circuits on the first \\texttt{True} value, raising \\texttt{GeneratorExit} to terminate the generator.\nBecause \\texttt{GeneratorExit} is a \\texttt{BaseException} subclass, the tracer records it as seven \\texttt{[ERROR] GeneratorExit} lines---even though the function executes correctly and the test passes.\nThe model observes these spurious error markers and reasonably but incorrectly predicts \\texttt{fail}.\nThis is not a model failure but a representation ceiling: the \\texttt{exception\\_only} format cannot distinguish genuine exceptions from benign \\texttt{BaseException} subclasses triggered by Python's iterator protocol.\n\nThese 2 irreducible errors establish a practical accuracy ceiling of $1 - 2/1190 = 99.83\\%$ for the \\texttt{exception\\_only} condition on this dataset, suggesting that further improvements would require either supplementary trace signals or modifications to the input representation.\n\n%% ============================================================\n\\section{Trace Length Ablation}\n\\label{sec:trace_length_full}\n\nTo test & ${<}$0.001*** \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}%\n}\n\\caption{Pairwise paired bootstrap comparisons for trace length ablation ($B{=}10{,}000$, seed=42). $\\Delta$ = accuracy difference between the two conditions (first $-$ second in the budget group; condition $-$ baseline in the other groups). **$p{<}0.01$; ***$p{<}0.001$.}\n\\label{tab:trace_length_pairwise}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\paragraph{Non-monotonic pattern.}\nThe trend across trace budgets is non-monotonic: 512 tokens (80.42\\%) slightly outperforms 256 tokens (78.82\\%), but doubling to 1024 tokens \\emph{decreases} accuracy to 77.23\\%---a statistically significant 3.19pp drop ($p{=}0.002$, 95\\% CI [$-$5.29, $-$1.09]).\nThe no-limit condition (81.76\\%) recovers and significantly exceeds 1024 tokens ($p{<}0.001$), but 1024 tokens is not significantly better than the \\texttt{no\\_trace} baseline (75.13\\%; $\\Delta{=}{+}2.10$pp, $p{=}0.078$, 95\\% CI [$-$0.17, $+$4.37]).\nThis means that truncating traces to an intermediate length can be \\emph{worse} than providing no trace at all---the 1024-token budget retains enough irrelevant content to mislead the model while discarding diagnostic events that might appear late in the trace.\n\n\\paragraph{Comparison to baselines.}\nAll four trace budget conditions significantly exceed \\texttt{no\\_trace} except 1024 tokens ($p{=}0.078$), confirming that raw trace content provides some signal but unreliably so.\nThe gap to \\texttt{exception\\_only} (99.83\\%) is massive for every budget condition (18--23pp, all $p{<}0.001$), underscoring that even unlimited raw traces cannot substitute for targeted signal extraction.\nThe best full-trace condition (no\\_limit, 81.76\\%) still lags \\texttt{exception\\_only} by 18.07pp (95\\% CI [$-$20.34, $-$15.88]).\n\n\\paragraph{Interpretation.}\nThese results rule out input truncation as the primary explanation for the full-trace performance gap: even with unlimited tokens, the model cannot extract the diagnostic signal that VOTA's targeted compression provides.\nThe non-monotonic dip at 1024 tokens is consistent with a ``worst-of-both-worlds'' regime where the model receives enough noisy context to dilute the signal but not enough to compensate through broader coverage.\nThe result reinforces the information density interpretation (\\S\\ref{sec:density}): the gap between full traces and VOTA is driven by the \\emph{content} of the trace (irrelevant lines diluting the diagnostic signal), not its \\emph{length}.\nIn contrast, VOTA's compressed representation concentrates the diagnostic signal into fewer tokens, making it sensitive to truncation when those tokens are removed (\\S\\ref{sec:cross_model}).\n\n%% ============================================================\n\\section{Calibration Analysis}\n\\label{sec:calibration_full}\n\nBeyond accuracy, we evaluate whether trace conditions affect prediction calibration, motivated by recent findings that code LLM confidence is often poorly calibrated \\citep{spiess2025calibration}.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lcc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Condition} & \\textbf{ECE $\\downarrow$} & \\textbf{Brier $\\downarrow$} \\\\\n\\midrule\nae\\_only & \\textbf{0.0004} & \\textbf{0.0008} \\\\\nexception\\_only & 0.0027 & 0.0013 \\\\\nVOTA & 0.0054 & 0.005 \\\\\nfull\\_trace & 0.096 & 0.144 \\\\\nno\\_trace & 0.171 & 0.197 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Calibration metrics on Qwen-2.5-7B ($N{=}1{,}190$, seed=42). ECE = expected calibration error; Brier = Brier score. Lower is better for both.}\n\\label{tab:calibration}\n\\end{table}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:calibration} shows that calibration quality mirrors the Tier~1/2 hierarchy.\nTier~1 conditions (\\texttt{ae\\_only}: ECE=0.0004; \\texttt{exception\\_only}: ECE=0.0027) produce near-perfectly calibrated predictions, while \\texttt{full\\_trace} (ECE=0.096) and \\texttt{no\\_trace} (ECE=0.171) exhibit substantial miscalibration.\nVOTA (ECE=0.0054) is well-calibrated but slightly worse than either Tier~1 component alone, reinforcing the pattern that combining trace signals dilutes rather than improves the prediction signal.\n\nThe Brier scores tell a consistent story: \\texttt{ae\\_only} (0.0008) and \\texttt{exception\\_only} (0.0013) achieve near-zero scores, while \\texttt{no\\_trace} (0.197) indicates substantial miscalibration.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig2_calibration}\n\\caption{Reliability diagrams for four trace conditions on Qwen-2.5-7B ($N{=}1{,}190$, seed=42). The Tier~1 condition (\\texttt{exception\\_only}) clusters near the diagonal (well-calibrated), while \\texttt{full\\_trace} and \\texttt{no\\_trace} exhibit systematic overconfidence.}\n\\label{fig:calibration}\n\\end{figure}\n\n%% ============================================================\n\\section{Cross-Dataset Generalization: DebugBench}\n\\label{sec:debugbench_full}\n\nTo test whether exception-based verdict prediction generalizes beyond LLM-generated solutions, we evaluate on DebugBench \\citep{tian2024debugbench}---a dataset of human-written programs with synthetically injected bugs (single-point mutations such as operator replacement, variable misuse, and missing statements).\nAfter filtering for executable Python samples with valid traces, we retain 426 samples with a near-balanced class distribution (majority baseline: 51.4\\%).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lcc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Condition} & \\textbf{Acc (\\%)} & \\textbf{95\\% CI} \\\\\n\\midrule\nexception\\_only & \\textbf{100.00} & [99.14, 100.00] \\\\\nVOTA & 99.77 & [99.30, 100.00] \\\\\nno\\_trace & 96.71 & [94.80, 98.40] \\\\\n\\midrule\nMajority baseline & 51.41 & --- \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Generalization to DebugBench ($N{=}426$, Qwen-2.5-7B, seed=42). Wilson 95\\% CIs. The component ranking (exception\\_only $\\geq$ VOTA $>$ no\\_trace) is preserved, and exception\\_only achieves perfect accuracy.}\n\\label{tab:debugbench}\n\\end{table}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:debugbench} shows that the component ranking from the main experiments is preserved: \\texttt{exception\\_only} (100.00\\%) $\\geq$ VOTA (99.77\\%) $>$ \\texttt{no\\_trace} (96.71\\%).\nThe \\texttt{exception\\_only} condition achieves perfect accuracy, confirming that the exception-based representation generalizes to a different fault distribution without degradation.\n\nThe absolute margins are much narrower than on the main dataset: the \\texttt{no\\_trace} baseline already achieves 96.71\\%---compared to 75.13\\% on HumanEval+MBPP---leaving only 3.29pp of headroom for trace-based improvement.\nThis ceiling effect reflects a fundamental difference in fault characteristics: DebugBench bugs are synthetically injected single-point mutations, which produce code that is structurally dissimilar from the original correct solution; a model can often identify the fault from source code alone.\nIn contrast, the HumanEval+MBPP solutions are LLM-generated, producing natural errors (logic mistakes, edge-case failures) where the faulty and correct solutions may be structurally similar, making code-only verdict prediction substantially harder.\n\nThese results support exception-based verdict prediction as a generalizable approach---the method does not degrade on an out-of-distribution dataset, and the exception dominance ranking (exception\\_only $\\geq$ VOTA $>$ no\\_trace) is consistent across both datasets---but the narrow DebugBench margins are insufficient to independently validate trace importance.\nThe 24.70pp trace lift on the main dataset (no\\_trace 75.13\\% $\\to$ exception\\_only 99.83\\%) remains the primary evidence for the value of exception-based trace signals.\n\n%% ============================================================\n\\section{Cross-Model Robustness Analysis}\n\\label{sec:robustness_analysis}\n\nThe cross-model gap pattern (Table~\\ref{tab:cross_model}) suggests a design principle: \\emph{trace representations should match the model capability floor, not the ceiling.}\nException events are consistently the most effective component on both models, and their structural simplicity---a fixed-format error message---ensures that the relative advantage over richer representations holds across all seeds.\nTwo of three DeepSeek seeds match Qwen-level accuracy (99.33\\%); the outlier (seed 123, 91.09\\%) is attributable to LoRA initialization landing in a suboptimal basin (fail recall = 70.5\\%), not to a failure of the exception signal itself---even this outlier outperforms VOTA (84.45\\%) by 6.64pp.\nThe VOTA cross-model gap is confounded by two factors: (1)~tokenizer efficiency---the DeepSeek tokenizer averages 2{,}626 tokens per VOTA sample versus Qwen's 1{,}929, causing 91.7\\% of training samples to exceed the 2{,}048-token context window; content-aware truncation preferentially removes the terminal structured prompt, preserving the verdict label but truncating trace content---and (2)~base model capability differences, consistent with findings that SFT generalization depends on pretrain quality \\citep{yang2024generalization}.\nThe two factors cannot be disentangled in the current experimental design.\nOur \\texttt{exception\\_only} design sidesteps the truncation confound entirely: inputs average ${\\sim}$200 tokens, well below the 2{,}048-token limit, so no training samples are truncated on either model.\nThe resulting 0.50pp cross-model gap (Table~\\ref{tab:cross_model}) is therefore a clean measure of model capability differences, making the ``capability floor'' principle directly testable.\nThe practical implication is that trace-based systems should default to the simplest sufficient signal; for smaller models, training stability (multi-seed selection, warmup schedules) is a secondary but addressable concern.\n\n%% ============================================================\n\\section{Training Dynamics and Loss Decoupling}\n\\label{sec:training_dynamics}\n\nThe DeepSeek \\texttt{exception\\_only} results reveal two instructive training dynamics.\nFirst, epoch-level analysis shows mild degradation in 2/3 seeds: seed 42 peaks at 99.83\\% after epoch~1 but settles at 99.33\\% by epoch~3 ($-$0.50pp), seed 123 degrades from 91.43\\% to 91.09\\% ($-$0.34pp), while seed 456 shows no epoch degradation (99.33\\% at both epoch~1 and epoch~3).\nEarly stopping may yield marginal gains for some seeds, but the epoch effect is small (${\\leq}$0.5pp) compared to the seed effect (8.24pp between seeds 42 and 123).\n\nSecond, seeds 123 and 456 converge to nearly identical final training losses (0.122 and 0.123 respectively), yet their accuracies differ by 8.24pp (91.09\\% vs.\\ 99.33\\%).\nThe critical difference lies in fail recall: seeds 42 and 456 achieve 100\\%, while seed 123 achieves only 70.5\\%---the LoRA initialization in seed 123 fails to learn the exception$\\to$fail mapping for a substantial fraction of failing samples, even though the per-token loss appears converged.\nThis demonstrates that token-level training loss can mask fundamental differences in sample-level classification ability, suggesting a parallel to the eval-loss/accuracy decoupling observed in DeepSeek VOTA (where eval loss rose 65\\% from epoch 1 to 3 while accuracy improved by 6.3pp) at the training phase.\nThe practical recommendation is twofold: (1)~use task-specific metrics (accuracy, fail recall) for checkpoint and seed selection rather than training or evaluation loss, and (2)~for deployment on smaller models, train multiple seeds and select the best, as a single run may not reliably converge to the correct classification boundary.\n\nThe contrast with VOTA is instructive: DeepSeek VOTA accuracy monotonically increases from 78.15\\% to 84.45\\% across epochs, but is consistent with a performance limit associated with the high trace truncation rate (\\S\\ref{sec:cross_model})---the model progressively extracts signal from samples with intact trace content but cannot fully compensate for the information loss in the truncated majority.\nThe 84.45\\% accuracy reflects a combination of trace information loss and model capability constraints.\n\n%% ============================================================\n\\section{Deduplication Statistics}\n\\label{sec:dedup_stats}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:dedup} reports the duplication rates across trace formats.\nBecause multiple passing solutions to the same problem produce identical (input, output) pairs in the \\texttt{exception\\_only} format---correct executions raise no exceptions, so the trace content is the same regardless of the solution's internal logic---the dataset contains duplicate rows.\nTrain--test input overlap is zero (split by problem ID), so no information leakage occurs.\nThe high duplication in \\texttt{exception\\_only} does not affect our experimental conclusions: after deduplication the training set still contains 2,858 unique samples and the test set 607, providing sufficient coverage for the 591-problem benchmark.\nThe \\texttt{exception\\_only} test set contains 48.99\\% duplicate inputs due to representation compactness; all confidence intervals treat samples as independent Bernoulli trials.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Split} & \\textbf{Total} & \\textbf{Unique} & \\textbf{Dup.\\ rate} \\\\\n\\midrule\nexception\\_only train & 4{,}720 & 2{,}858 & 39.45\\% \\\\\nexception\\_only test  & 1{,}190 &   607 & 48.99\\% \\\\\nraw\\_traces (full)    & 5{,}910 & 5{,}803 &  1.81\\% \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Deduplication statistics. The \\texttt{exception\\_only} format collapses multiple passing solutions into identical rows because correct executions produce no exception events. Train--test input overlap is 0 (no leakage).}\n\\label{tab:dedup}\n\\end{table}\n\n%% ============================================================\n\\section{Regex Baseline Analysis}\n\\label{sec:regex_baselines}\n\nTo establish a non-learning upper bound, we evaluate five regex-based baselines on the \\texttt{exception\\_only} test set ($N{=}1{,}190$) and two format-specific baselines on VOTA and \\texttt{full\\_trace} inputs.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{llc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Baseline} & \\textbf{Method} & \\textbf{Acc (\\%)} \\\\\n\\midrule\nB1: \\texttt{[ERROR]} presence & string match & 93.45 \\\\\nB2: Traceback presence & string match & 71.51 \\\\\nB3: Any keyword & union of B1+B2+Exception & 93.45 \\\\\nB4: Exception type regex & regex on exception class names & \\textbf{93.61} \\\\\nB5: Format marker & \\texttt{[No exception data]} $\\to$ pass & 99.33$^\\ddag$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nVOTA format regex & best regex on VOTA traces & 75.55 \\\\\nfull\\_trace format regex & best regex on full traces & 77.39 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Regex baseline accuracy ($N{=}1{,}190$). B4 is the strongest legitimate baseline (6.22pp below SFT \\texttt{exception\\_only}, McNemar $p{<}0.001$). $^\\ddag$B5 exploits a data formatting artifact: the \\texttt{[No exception data in trace]} marker is deterministically present in all pass samples, making this a format-level shortcut rather than a genuine exception-based heuristic.}\n\\label{tab:regex}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe strongest legitimate baseline (B4, 93.61\\%) applies regex pattern-matching on Python exception class names (e.g., \\texttt{ValueError}, \\texttt{TypeError}).\nIts errors concentrate on 8 boundary samples where a \\texttt{ValueError} is raised inside a \\texttt{try/except} block and caught---the program passes despite an exception occurring in the trace.\nSFT \\texttt{exception\\_only} correctly classifies 6 of these 8 samples, indicating that the model learns the semantic distinction between caught (benign) and uncaught (fault-indicating) exceptions.\nB5 (99.33\\%) achieves near-SFT accuracy but relies on the deterministic presence of a format marker in pass-class traces, which is an artifact of the trace preprocessing pipeline rather than a generalizable signal; McNemar test vs.\\ SFT yields $p{=}0.041$, confirming a statistically significant difference.\n",

      "totalLines": 484
    }
